because the balance is taken across an instantaneous transition
\citep{Stewart2000RigidImpact}.  The block
\(J_+^{\mathsf T}WJ_+\) is the generalized inertia seen in the outgoing
velocity directions, \(C_+^{\mathsf T}\) places the unknown constraint
impulses into those directions, and \(C_+\) imposes the required outgoing
velocity relations.  The right-hand side carries the known incoming momentum
into the same directions.

The resulting event state must still respect the unilateral character of
any active stop.  With \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{stop}}\) signed positive toward
increasing \(s\), an impulse at the fully open stop or engaged low-ratio
seat must satisfy
\(\Gamma_{\mathrm{stop}}\geq0\), whereas the upper primary stop can act
only toward decreasing \(s\) and therefore requires
\(\Gamma_{\mathrm{stop}}\leq0\).  A solution requiring the opposite sign
cannot be supplied by that stop, so the proposed outgoing constraint is
not mechanically admissible.
% F10

\paragraph{Completing the upper primary stop} The final system can now resolve the secondary-speed
question posed at the outset. Take \(J_-=J_+=J_{\mathrm{eng}}\) and first
consider the case in which the belt supplies no angular impulse to the
secondary.  The shaft-side and movable-side secondary rotations contribute
the following \((\omega_s,\dot s)\) block to the generalized inertia matrix:

\begin{equation}
    \left[
        J_{\mathrm{eng}}^{\mathsf T}
        W
        J_{\mathrm{eng}}
    \right]_{\omega_s,\dot s}^{\mathrm{secondary\ rotation}}
    =
    \begin{bmatrix}
        I_{\mathrm B,s}+I_{s,F}+I_{s,M}
        &
        I_{s,M}\dfrac{d\theta_s}{ds}
        \\[6pt]
        I_{s,M}\dfrac{d\theta_s}{ds}
        &
        I_{s,M}
        \left(\dfrac{d\theta_s}{ds}\right)^2
    \end{bmatrix}.
    \label{eq:finite_event-secondary-helix-inertia-block}
\end{equation}

The rows identify the retained direction in which momentum is counted,
while the columns identify the retained velocity contributing that
momentum. The upper-left entry therefore describes the contribution from
\(\omega_s\) to momentum in the \(\omega_s\) direction: both secondary
assemblies rotate with the shaft. The upper-right entry describes the
contribution from \(\dot s\) to that same momentum, because shift rotates
the movable assembly through the helix. The lower-left entry is the
reciprocal coupling, in which shaft rotation contributes to the
shift-direction momentum. The lower-right entry describes the contribution
from \(\dot s\) to the shift-direction momentum, giving the movable
secondary's rotational inertia reflected through the helix into shift.

At the upper stop, the constraint row in
\Cref{eq:finite_event_kkt_system} imposes \(\dot s^+=0\).  The outgoing
terms multiplied by \(\dot s^+\) therefore vanish, while the incoming
shift-induced rotation remains in the known right-hand side.  With no
secondary belt impulse, the \(\omega_s\) row of the same system becomes

\begin{equation}
    \left(
        I_{\mathrm B,s}+I_{s,F}+I_{s,M}
    \right)\omega_s^+
    =
    \left(
        I_{\mathrm B,s}+I_{s,F}+I_{s,M}
    \right)\omega_s^-
    +
    I_{s,M}\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}\dot s^- .
    \label{eq:finite-event-upper-stop-secondary-row}
\end{equation}

The final term on the right is the angular momentum carried into the event
by the movable secondary's helix-relative rotation.  Using

\begin{equation}
    \omega_{s,M}^-
    =
    \omega_s^-
    +
    \frac{d\theta_s}{ds}\dot s^- ,
    \label{eq:finite-event-secondary-movable-incoming-speed}
\end{equation}

the same row gives

\begin{equation}
    \omega_s^+
    =
    \frac{
        (I_{\mathrm B,s}+I_{s,F})\omega_s^-
        +
        I_{s,M}\omega_{s,M}^-
    }{
        I_{\mathrm B,s}+I_{s,F}+I_{s,M}
    }.
    \label{eq:finite-event-upper-stop-common-speed}
\end{equation}

The common secondary speed introduced at the beginning of the boundary
example has therefore emerged directly from
\Cref{eq:finite_event_kkt_system}.  The incoming helix-relative motion is
carried into the solve through the off-diagonal coupling, while the stop
condition removes that relative motion from the outgoing state.  The
pin--track impulse that transfers angular momentum between the shaft-side
and movable-side assemblies is internal to the engaged connection; its
equal-and-opposite actions cancel from the combined \(\omega_s\) balance
rather than appearing as another component of
\(\boldsymbol\Gamma\).

\begin{remarkbox}[Remark (Energy consistency check)]

The completed event solution also provides a useful consistency check.
Because the physical velocities on both sides are already known from
\(J_-\boldsymbol\nu^-\) and \(J_+\boldsymbol\nu^+\), the kinetic energy
before and after the transition follows directly:

\begin{equation}
    T^-
    =
    \frac{1}{2}
    (J_-\boldsymbol\nu^-)^{\mathsf T}
    W
    (J_-\boldsymbol\nu^-),
    \qquad
    T^+
    =
    \frac{1}{2}
    (J_+\boldsymbol\nu^+)^{\mathsf T}
    W
    (J_+\boldsymbol\nu^+).
    \label{eq:finite_event_kinetic_energies}
\end{equation}

For the perfectly inelastic event model, the solved transition should not
increase the total kinetic energy.  This follows directly from the event
balance.  Premultiplying the first relation in
\Cref{eq:finite_event_generalized_impulse_momentum} by
\((\boldsymbol\nu^+)^{\mathsf T}\) gives

\[
    (J_+\boldsymbol\nu^+)^{\mathsf T}\Delta\boldsymbol p
    =
    (C_+\boldsymbol\nu^+)^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol\Gamma
    =
    0,
\]

because the outgoing solution satisfies
\(C_+\boldsymbol\nu^+=0\).  Substituting the momentum change from
\Cref{eq:finite_event_body_momentum_change} then gives

\begin{equation}
    T^- - T^+
    =
    \frac{1}{2}
    (J_+\boldsymbol\nu^+-J_-\boldsymbol\nu^-)^{\mathsf T}
    W
    (J_+\boldsymbol\nu^+-J_-\boldsymbol\nu^-)
    \geq 0.
    \label{eq:finite_event_energy_loss}
\end{equation}

The right-hand side is a sum of one half of each retained mass or inertia
times the square of its physical velocity change.  The result therefore
provides a direct check that the event solution is dissipative even when
the kinematic connection changes.  Individual bodies can still gain kinetic
energy through the momentum exchange, as the movable secondary does at the
upper stop; only the total must not increase.

\end{remarkbox}


\section{Evaluation of the Complete System}
\label{sec:complete_system_evaluation}

The derivation is now complete. Beginning with a single pulley's geometry,
we have brought the shafts, sheaves, and belt into one coupled description,
including the changes in contact and support encountered as they move.
We can now step back from the individual balances and see how they work
together to follow the CVT through time.

\paragraph{Establishing the initial condition.}
We first choose \(\vect{x}_0\) to describe the starting configuration and
motion for the case being studied. That choice must respect the travel
limits in \Cref{eq:shift_operating_domain} and the velocity constraints of
the contacts and supports present. In the deadzone, for example, the belt
must move with the secondary: \(v_b=r_{s,\mathrm{eff,dz}}\omega_s\), as
required by \Cref{eq:deadzone_secondary_lock}. The shift position restricts
the arrangements in \Cref{tab:shift_structural_arrangements}, while nonzero
relative speed identifies an existing contact as sliding. Zero relative
speed alone does not establish sticking; the required traction must still
be available. Likewise, a sheave at rest against a stop can remain there
only if the stop can supply the necessary reaction. The contact and support
checks in
\Cref{sec:contact_admissibility_transitions,sec:shift_regime_transitions}
settle these remaining choices under the initial shaft loading, establishing
the initial regime \(q_0\) that accompanies the prescribed state.

\paragraph{Evaluating the current regime.}
With \(q\) established, we hold it fixed while calculating how the CVT
accelerates. The geometry and actuator maps are evaluated at the current
state, while the shaft interfaces supply the applied torques and attached
inertias. For engaged motion, the \(6\times6\) system in
\Cref{eq:engaged_six_by_six_mechanical_core} gathers the mechanics of
\Crefrange{sec:geometry}{sec:belt_transport_contact} into one coupled
response. In the deadzone, \Cref{eq:deadzone_linear_mechanical_core} is used
instead. At a supported stop, \(\ddot s=0\) is prescribed, so the supported
member's axial balance determines the force needed to hold it there rather
than its acceleration, as summarized in \Cref{tab:structural_stop_reactions}.

Within the engaged system, two sliding contacts supply both traction
utilizations through \Cref{eq:sliding_traction_utilization}, leaving one
linear solve. One sticking contact requires the scalar root in
\Cref{eq:sticking_traction_root_condition}, and two require the coupled root
in \Cref{eq:stick_stick_root_condition}. Each trial is evaluated through the
same active linear system, and the root is reached when the returned
accelerations preserve the speed match at every sticking contact. The solved
accelerations and the stored shift velocity \(\dot s\) then complete the
state derivative \(\dot{\vect{x}}\) in \Cref{eq:state-derivative}.

\paragraph{Checking whether the regime can continue.}
As the integrator advances the state using this derivative, the contact and
support conditions determine whether the same regime can be retained. The
tangential checks in \Cref{sec:contact_admissibility_transitions} watch for
a sliding relative speed reaching zero or a sticking traction reaching its
static limit. The shift checks in \Cref{sec:shift_regime_transitions} locate
arrivals at travel boundaries and monitor the stop reactions. A stop releases
when its required reaction crosses zero, since holding the mechanism there
would then require the stop to pull. Primary separation requires both zero
primary normal load and an opening tendency in the contact-free primary
balance, as in \Cref{eq:primary_separation_conditions}. The engaged belt
must also satisfy \Cref{eq:wrap_contact_admissibility}: negative tension or
local normal loading would indicate slack or lift-off, neither of which
this belt representation describes.

If a regime event occurs, the integrator advances only to that instant.
Before it can continue, the transition must establish both the motion
immediately afterward and the regime that can sustain it. If the proposed
regime admits the incoming physical motion, the state remains continuous;
otherwise, \Cref{eq:finite_event_kkt_system} supplies the outgoing velocities
and impulses using the connections on both sides. Any stop impulse must act
in the direction in which the stop can push. The continuous mechanics are
then evaluated at that outgoing state to test whether the proposed contacts
and supports can be maintained. At zero relative speed, sticking is tested
first; if it is unavailable, the sliding branch must produce the direction
of relative motion it assumes
(\Cref{eq:sliding_onset_sign_consistency}). Simultaneous changes are checked
as one combined regime, since they affect the same mechanical response.

\paragraph{Advancing and repeating.}
With no intervening event, the integrator advances the state using
evaluations of \(\dot{\vect{x}}\) and retains \(q\). After a transition,
it resumes at the event time from the accepted outgoing state and regime.
In either case, the next evaluation begins with an updated state and an
established regime, and the cycle repeats. Together, the continuous balances
and transition laws thus determine the CVT's motion from the prescribed
initial condition and shaft loading.

\chapter{Results and Discussion}
\label{chap:results}

The formulation in \Cref{chap:derivation} determines the CVT response from
its internal mechanics and the loading at its shafts. The numerical
investigation begins with verification of this coupled model and comparison
with an independent transient formulation. It then examines when the retained
actuator and belt dynamics change the predicted behaviour. Full transient
simulations finally bring these mechanisms together, following how the CVT
engages, shifts, and responds to changing loads.

\section{Computational Implementation and Reproducibility}
\label{sec:results_implementation_reproducibility}

CINDER is the simulator developed in this work to implement the formulation
and carry out these numerical studies. Its numerical methods are documented in
\Cref{app:numerical_implementation}.

The model was developed around a Baja SAE CVT, whose hardware forms the basis
of the reference configuration in
\Cref{app:reference_configuration}. Much of the investigation therefore
concerns the operating range of a small off-road vehicle. The quantitative
importance of the effects examined here requires separate assessment for
substantially different machines.

Complete study definitions, including the physical configurations and numerical
settings, are collected in \Cref{app:study_reproducibility}. The external
systems that drive and load the CVT, including simplified engine and vehicle
models, are defined separately in \Cref{app:results_boundary_models}.

The results reported here use a frozen CINDER release and a separate frozen
study record containing the case definitions, analysis scripts, and outputs.
Both records are identified in \Cref{tab:results_reproducibility_record}.
The correspondence between the archived outputs and the manuscript figures
and tables is given in \Cref{app:artifact_index}.

\section{Internal Verification}
\label{sec:results_verification}

The same belt--pulley contact forces enter the belt transport, shaft rotation,
and axial sheave balances. Their numerical evaluation must preserve these
coupled effects as contacts stick or slide and travel constraints engage or
release. Verification therefore covers both the individual mechanical
conditions and their combined response: the operating-domain audit and
work--energy balance check local and global consistency, while refinement of
the time solution and examination of the instantaneous contact closure assess
the numerical reliability of that response.

\subsection{Mechanical Invariants and Operating-Domain Audit}
\label{sec:results_mechanical_invariants}

The audit combines the ten-second, flat-road Baja launch with constructed
constant torque--inertia cases that exercise additional contact and structural
regimes. The launch retains the engine and vehicle boundaries of
\Cref{app:primary_engine_boundary,app:secondary_vehicle_boundary}; the
constructed cases instead prescribe the torque and attached inertia at each
shaft using \Cref{app:fixed_shaft_boundary}, while allowing both shaft speeds
to evolve. All use the reference CVT and its zero-clearance bilateral secondary
helix, defined in
\Cref{app:common_physical_configuration,app:reference_helix_topology}.
Case definitions and audit thresholds are collected in
\Cref{app:study_mechanical_invariants}.

All 25 required coverage classes were exercised. These include rest and
deadzone motion, engagement, free shifting in both directions, the travel
stops, forward and reverse rotation, either slipping contact with either slip
sign, and all four both-slip sign combinations. Across 6,257 audited states
and 81 hybrid transitions, the recorded mechanical checks passed, including
those applied to the exact post-transition states. The largest row-scaled
mechanical-closure residual was \(1.11\times10^{-14}\), using the
normalization in \Cref{eq:invariants_scaled_closure_residual}. The coverage
record is given in \Cref{tab:invariants_coverage_classes};
\Cref{fig:verification_contact_regimes} shows how differently the canonical
contact cases behave within that successful audit.

% FIGURE 1 -- Assemble/replot existing evidence, not a new simulation.
% Source: CINDER_v1.1.2_mechanical_invariants_recap.pdf, pp. 5, 8, 9.
% (a) Requested-branch dwell for the ten canonical contact cases (p. 8).
%     Mark secondary-slip (+) and both-slip (-,+) as targeted initial states.
%     Mark the 30 ms no-exit cases as lower bounds, NOT measured exit times.
% (b) Minimum distributed wrap normal for each initialized case (p. 9).
%     These are minima over the audited case, not necessarily only its first branch.
% Do not replace (b) with an invented normal-load profile or imply that minima
% of different quantities occur at the same time. Keep the 25-class list in the appendix.
\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \CINDERVerificationFigure[60mm]{figures/results/verification/contact_regimes.pdf}{%
    (a) Canonical contact-case dwell, with targeted states and the 30 ms limit marked.\par
    (b) Minimum local wrap-normal loading for the same initialized cases.\par
    Source: mechanical-invariants recap, pages 8--9; values on page 5.}
  \caption{Contact-regime coverage and persistence in the reference CVT.
  (a) Time to the first transition from each requested initial contact state;
  a case with no transition in the 30 ms interval has a dwell of at least
  30 ms. Slip signs refer to \(v_{\mathrm{rel},j}\) in
  \Cref{eq:tangential_relative_speed}. (b) Minimum distributed normal loading
  on each pulley over the corresponding audited case. The contact cases
  satisfy the retained constraints, but differ substantially in persistence
  and local contact margin.}
  \label{fig:verification_contact_regimes}
\end{figure}

Several requested branches persist throughout the 30 ms interval, whereas
secondary-slip \((+)\) and both-slip \((-,+)\) leave their initial branches
through secondary resticking after approximately \(414~\mu\mathrm{s}\) and
\(9.82~\mu\mathrm{s}\), respectively. Both required targeted initial states
in this reference configuration. The readily reproduced both-slip \((+,+)\)
case also resticks quickly, after \(31.7~\mu\mathrm{s}\). Thus short dwell
and difficulty finding an initial state are distinct observations. In these
cases the coupled dynamics rapidly bring the secondary relative speed back
to zero; a brief initial branch is a resolved part of the trajectory rather
than a failure to continue the simulation. These observations describe the
selected hardware, loads, and initial states, not the frequency of these
regimes in vehicle operation generally.

The local contact margins in \Cref{fig:verification_contact_regimes}(b) also
show why integrated clamping alone is an incomplete admissibility check. In
the case initialized in both-slip \((-,-)\), the minimum primary distributed
normal loading is \(0.0482~\mathrm{N/rad}\), while the integrated primary
normal resultant remains at least \(374~\mathrm{N}\) over that case. The
small local margin is therefore not accompanied by disappearance of the
overall normal load. The retained full-wrap description requires nonnegative
contact loading everywhere along the wrap as well as a nonnegative resultant.
Together with the exact successor checks, this audit demonstrates consistent
operation across the tested regime set, without implying that every admissible
initial state or every possible transition has been exhausted.
\FloatBarrier

\subsection{Energy Consistency}
\label{sec:results_energy_consistency}

The energy audit follows the reference launch for ten seconds on flat road,
with the numerical settings in \Cref{app:study_energy_consistency}. Its
inventory includes the retained kinetic stores and conservative
actuator-spring energy. The boundary
work is the signed net work supplied at the two shafts,
\begin{equation}
  W_{\mathrm B}(t)
  =\int_0^t
    \left(\tau_{\mathrm B,p}\omega_p+
          \tau_{\mathrm B,s}\omega_s\right)\dd t,
  \label{eq:verification_boundary_work}
\end{equation}
so a resisting vehicle load contributes negative work. The audited balance is
\begin{equation}
  W_{\mathrm B}(t)
  =\Delta E_{\mathrm{stored}}(t)
   +E_{\mathrm{slip}}(t)+E_{\mathrm{capture}}(t)+R_E(t),
  \label{eq:verification_energy_balance}
\end{equation}
where \(R_E\) is the unexplained numerical residual, not an additional loss
mechanism.

\Cref{fig:verification_energy_balance} compares this accounting on the
throughput scale and then resolves the much smaller balance defect. The final
residual is \(0.0254~\mathrm{J}\) against \(52.17~\mathrm{kJ}\) of net
boundary work, giving \(\epsilon_E=4.88\times10^{-7}\) under
\Cref{eq:energy_final_balance_fraction}. The final energy ledger in
\Cref{tab:verification_energy_ledger} distinguishes the two retained
irreversible channels from stored energy: kinetic belt--pulley slip accounts
for approximately \(1.588~\mathrm{kJ}\), whereas the cumulative inelastic
capture loss is only \(0.254~\mathrm{J}\) in this trajectory. Spring storage
and inertial storage are not counted as dissipation. The omitted mechanisms
remain those stated in \AssRef{assump:no_parasitic_losses}; this ledger
quantifies the losses represented by the model.

% FIGURE 2 -- Assemble existing plots from the energy recap.
% Source: CINDER_v1.1.2_energy_audit_interpretation.pdf, pp. 2--4.
% (a) W_B and Delta E_stored + E_slip + E_capture, in kJ (p. 2).
% (b) Signed raw residual history, in J (p. 3), retaining all event-scale features.
% (c) Summed continuous defect versus audit spacing squared (p. 4).
% The 0.024510 J line is a zero-spacing extrapolate, not an error bar and not
% a direct estimate of the quadrature error remaining at the finest spacing.
% The channel values are tabulated below; p. 8 is an optional channel-history view.
\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \CINDERVerificationFigure[62mm]{figures/results/verification/energy_balance.pdf}{%
    (a) Cumulative net work and accounted energy.\par
    (b) Signed energy-residual history on a joule-scale axis.\par
    (c) Continuous-defect refinement against audit spacing squared.\par
    Source: energy-audit recap, pages 2--4.}
  \caption{Mechanical-energy accounting for the reference launch.
  (a) Net boundary work and stored-energy change plus modeled dissipation.
  (b) Their signed residual, shown separately from the kilojoule-scale
  throughput. (c) Refinement of the summed continuous-segment defect using
  audit spacings of 5, 2.5, 1.25, and 0.625 ms; the horizontal line is the
  Richardson zero-spacing estimate. Capture losses are accounted for at the
  discrete events rather than integrated across the velocity resets.}
  \label{fig:verification_energy_balance}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[H]
  \centering
  \small
  \caption{Final energy ledger for the reference audit. The residual is
  evaluated from unrounded quantities; displayed entries are rounded.}
  \label{tab:verification_energy_ledger}
  \begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}
    \toprule
    Quantity & Energy [J] \\
    \midrule
    Net boundary work & \(52\,168.893\) \\
    Stored-energy change & \(50\,580.274\) \\
    Kinetic-slip dissipation & \(1\,588.340\) \\
    Inelastic capture dissipation & \(0.253859\) \\
    Balance residual & \(0.025444\) \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

The refinement panel is important because coincident cumulative curves alone
would conceal a small systematic defect. Reducing the quadrature spacing
approaches a finite continuous-defect estimate of \(0.024510~\mathrm{J}\):
post-processing refinement alone does not drive the fixed-trajectory residual
to zero. A tighter trajectory integration gives a final residual of
\(-0.022843~\mathrm{J}\), changing its sign while retaining the same small
scale. The maximum normalized final-state change is
\(1.30\times10^{-7}\), using
\Cref{eq:energy_final_state_normalization}. Discrete captures separately
satisfy the recorded energy-drop relation to within
\(2.27\times10^{-13}~\mathrm{J}\). These checks support energy consistency
at the observed numerical-error scale across both the continuous motion and
the discrete updates. The residual fraction measures accounting accuracy; it
is not a transmission-efficiency estimate.
\FloatBarrier

\subsection{Solver Convergence}
\label{sec:results_solver_convergence}

A hybrid trajectory includes the contact history as well as the shaft speeds
and shift motion. Its numerical convergence therefore requires both parts to
agree. The convergence benchmark uses the ten-second reference launch, with a
tight LSODA solution at \(\mathrm{rtol}=10^{-7}\),
\(\mathrm{atol}=10^{-10}\), and a 2.5 ms maximum step as the numerical
reference. The physical case and tested settings are specified in
\Cref{app:study_solver_convergence}.

The transition signature records the ordered events, successor modes, and
occurrence of velocity resets. State differences are evaluated between
corresponding smooth segments after matching that signature; a different
signature is itself a convergence failure. Each segment is sampled at the
same fractional progress between its endpoint events rather than at the same
absolute time, so a small event-time offset does not compare a pre-reset state
with a post-reset state. Event-time
error is reported separately, together with the fraction of time spent in
different regimes. The normalization and duration-weighted root-mean-square (RMS) error
are defined by
\Cref{eq:solver_state_scale,eq:solver_normalized_state_error,eq:solver_event_aligned_rms}.
The acceptance criteria in \Cref{tab:solver_convergence_guards} require an
exact transition signature as well as bounds on the state and timing errors.

% FIGURE 3 -- Assemble existing formal-grid and absolute-tolerance evidence.
% Source: CINDER_v1.1.2_Solver_Convergence_Final_Recap(1).pdf, pp. 3--4.
% (a) Formal 8 x 5 error grid; mark the research point and all-guards-passing cells.
% (b) Independent atol error plot; annotate reference-signature recovery if
%     adding labels from the reported seven-point sweep. Do not interpret
%     event count alone as the full transition-signature test.
% Keep the grid as sampled cells; do not interpolate a sharper pass/fail boundary.
\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \CINDERVerificationFigure[60mm]{figures/results/verification/solver_refinement.pdf}{%
    (a) Formal relative-tolerance / maximum-step convergence grid.\par
    (b) Independent absolute-tolerance sweep.\par
    Mark the research setting and distinguish state error from all-guard acceptance.\par
    Source: solver-convergence recap, pages 3--4.}
  \caption{Convergence of the reference hybrid trajectory.
  (a) Event-aligned normalized state error over the formal relative-tolerance
  and maximum-step grid. (b) Independent absolute-tolerance sensitivity at
  \(\mathrm{rtol}=10^{-4}\) and a 10 ms maximum step. The research setting
  is \(\mathrm{rtol}=10^{-4}\), \(\mathrm{atol}=10^{-7}\), and a
  10 ms maximum step. The maximum step is an upper bound on the adaptive
  integration, not a fixed time increment.}
  \label{fig:verification_solver_refinement}
\end{figure}

\Cref{fig:verification_solver_refinement}(a) shows a contiguous passing
region: every tested maximum step from 5 to 100 ms satisfies all criteria
when \(\mathrm{rtol}\leq10^{-4}\), with
\(\mathrm{atol}=10^{-3}\mathrm{rtol}\) in that sweep. At the research
setting, the event-aligned trajectory RMS is \(4.27\times10^{-7}\), the
maximum normalized state error is \(6.96\times10^{-5}\), and the largest
corresponding event-time difference is \(7.99~\mu\mathrm{s}\). The complete
16-transition signature is reproduced, and the regime-mismatch fraction is
\(8.46\times10^{-6}\).

The independent absolute-tolerance sweep in
\Cref{fig:verification_solver_refinement}(b) matters because the angular
speeds, shift position, and shift speed have different numerical scales.
Loose absolute tolerances alter the transition history; the tighter tested
range from \(3\times10^{-7}\) to \(10^{-8}\) recovers the reference
history, with \(10^{-7}\) satisfying all criteria. The one failed formal
integration uses the coarsest combination,
\(\mathrm{rtol}=10^{-2}\) and a 100 ms maximum step, and reaches an
inadmissible primary flyweight-contact state. This is a numerical/domain
failure of that calculation, not evidence of physical lift-off on the
converged trajectory.

% FIGURE 4 -- Ready existing evidence, not an invented two-run example.
% Source: solver-convergence recap, p. 9, Figure 8:
% gross trajectory error vs native solver points, grouped by transition count.
% Gross metric includes omega_p, omega_s, v_b, s; excludes sdot and is NOT the
% event-aligned five-state RMS used for formal convergence.
% A more intuitive trajectory + regime-timeline replacement remains optional,
% pending selection of a specific DIFFERENT-signature run from archived outputs.
% The p. 8 Figure 7 overlay is SAME-signature and must not be used to claim a
% missed transition. Keep data/normalization consistent if replacing this slot.
\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \CINDERVerificationFigure{figures/results/verification/gross_motion_hybrid_history.pdf}{%
    Gross trajectory error versus native solver points, grouped by transition count.\par
    Source: solver-convergence recap, page 9, Figure 8.\par
    This existing population plot supports the current text without selecting a new case.}
  \caption{Macroscopic agreement and hybrid-history agreement are distinct.
  The dense exploratory sweep is shown using the absolute-time gross-state
  error for \((\omega_p,\omega_s,v_b,s)\), excluding \(\dot s\), and
  grouped by transition count. A low gross error need not reproduce the
  reference contact history. Native solver points are a work proxy; they are
  not right-hand-side evaluation counts.}
  \label{fig:verification_gross_hybrid_agreement}
\end{figure}

The dense sweep makes the distinction visible in
\Cref{fig:verification_gross_hybrid_agreement}. Among 609 completed runs
with a different transition signature, the median absolute-time gross-state
RMS error is only \(1.23\times10^{-4}\). This diagnostic includes shaft
speeds, belt speed, and shift position, but excludes shift speed; it is not
the event-aligned five-state metric used for acceptance. Gross motion can
remain close while brief contact transitions differ. Resolving internal
traction and shift mechanisms therefore requires the hybrid checks, not only
a visually converged speed or ratio curve. The demonstrated tolerance range
applies to this benchmark; settings for other cases remain part of their
individual study definitions.

% native-point comparison from recap p. 10, with hardware and timing-scope context.

\subsection{Closure Conditioning and Root Structure}
\label{sec:results_closure_structure}

The engaged solve separates into a linear mechanical response at fixed
\((\lambda_p,\lambda_s)\) and a nonlinear selection of the traction required
by sticking contact. The eight-variable form, including the retained pulley
torques, is given in \Cref{eq:appendix_eight_unknown_closure}. These two
levels are assessed separately: uniqueness of the fixed-traction response
does not imply uniqueness of the traction pair that enforces stick.

At the frozen states drawn from the reference launch and constructed
operating cases, no rank-deficient mechanical system is found in the sampled
static-capacity or expanded traction maps. Each sampled fixed-traction system
therefore has a unique linear response. The condition numbers in
\Cref{fig:verification_closure_robustness}(a) use the equilibrated matrix of
\Cref{eq:appendix_equilibrated_system}; the largest reported value among the
four representative static-capacity maps is \(3.38\times10^5\).
Full rank does not imply uniform sensitivity, and some trial points violate
contact or support admissibility even within the static-capacity box. The
map domains and mechanical-admissibility checks are distinguished in
\Cref{app:study_closure_conditioning}.

% low_ratio_seat 1.33e4; mid_shift 3.70e4; upper_stop 3.38e5;

% Do not draw iteration paths: the recap records starting points/outcomes, not paths.
% Keep the reported 375 TOTAL attempts distinct from the current appendix's
% 7x7/9x9 manifest settings; the archived run specification needs reconciliation.
\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \CINDERVerificationFigure[60mm]{figures/results/verification/closure_robustness.pdf}{%
    (a) Four representative static-capacity-map maxima of equilibrated
    \(\kappa(\vect A_s)\), replotted from the closure recap, page 3.\par
    (b) Mid-shift multistart outcomes, closure recap, page 5.\par
    Distinguish accepted roots from unsuccessful starts.}
  \caption{Representative fixed-traction and sticking-root behaviour.
  (a) Maximum equilibrated mechanical-matrix condition number in each of four
  sampled static-capacity maps; these are map maxima rather than values at
  the accepted sticking root. (b) Starting guesses and acceptance outcomes
  for the mid-shift sticking solve. Accepted solutions belong to one root
  cluster; unsuccessful starts are shown separately. Mechanical admissibility
  is assessed independently of membership in the static-capacity box.}
  \label{fig:verification_closure_robustness}
\end{figure}

The representative nonlinear solves are also reproducible across starting
estimates. Across 15 states and 375 multistart attempts in total, all accepted
solutions for each state belong to one physical root cluster.
\Cref{fig:verification_closure_robustness}(b) illustrates this result at
mid-shift. The statement concerns the accepted roots: it neither assumes
that every initial guess succeeds nor proves nonlinear uniqueness outside
the tested states.

Targeted continuation reveals a different structure elsewhere in the
reduced contact equations. In the selected stick--stick example of
\Cref{fig:verification_closure_fold}, a continuous solution branch turns back
in loading space. A fixed loading then intersects that branch twice,
producing two traction pairs that satisfy both sticking residuals and the
physical admissibility checks. The smaller static-capacity reserve of the
selected pair is \(0.098\), so the multiplicity is not explained simply by
including an above-capacity mathematical root. It occurs in the nonlinear
traction selection while the fixed-traction mechanics still give a definite
response at either root.

% FIGURE 6 -- Existing selected stick-stick example, recap p. 6.
% Case: sf0p950_sdotm40_rpm3000.
% Use (a) focused lambda-plane view and (b) one-dimensional fold/crossing view.
% Retain the same root colours/identifiers and load slice in both panels.
% The 3-D LOCAL CURVE on p. 6 is optional, not a required extra panel.
% Do not relabel its continuation coordinate as a physical state variable.
% The mixed scalar example on pp. 8--9 remains supporting evidence for a later
% appendix slot; it is not substituted for this two-contact example.
\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \CINDERVerificationFigure[60mm]{figures/results/verification/sticking_closure_fold.pdf}{%
    (a) Focused traction-plane view with two admissible stick--stick roots.\par
    (b) Continuation curve and fixed-load line intersecting both sides of the fold.\par
    Source: closure recap, page 6; selected late-shift opening case.}
  \caption{Two admissible sticking solutions in a deliberately selected
  frozen-state example. The traction-plane view identifies the two roots;
  the continuation view shows that they lie on opposite sides of one folded
  branch at the same applied loading. Multiple nonlinear sticking roots can
  therefore coexist with a unique mechanical response for each prescribed
  traction pair.}
  \label{fig:verification_closure_fold}
\end{figure}

Multiplicity also occurs in a mixed branch with primary slip at
\(-\mu_k\) and secondary stick. The targeted 62-state investigation found
one distinct scalar-fold geometry, with secondary sticking roots near
\(0.434\) and \(0.546\) on the selected loading slice. One approaches
local wrap-contact loss. The examples establish that neither the two-contact
nor the one-contact sticking problem is globally single-rooted; their
frequency in this targeted search is not an operating probability.

These folded branches were exposed by deliberate frozen-state searches.
No spontaneous ambiguity of this type was observed in the maintained
transient studies. During smooth evolution, the continuation strategy in
\Cref{app:num_contact_continuation} reuses the previous accepted traction
solution, encouraging the solver to remain on the nearby branch. Fresh
mechanical-residual and admissibility checks still apply, as described in
\Cref{app:num_contact_search}. Continuation reduces arbitrary root changes
but cannot remove a fold or guarantee a unique continuation when a branch
ends. The positive sampled solvability results and the global multiplicity
finding therefore describe different, compatible properties of the reduced
closure.
