Arrival time remains a useful consequence of these differences, but it is not
the organizing quantity for the comparison. The more useful question is what
changed inside the transmission when two vehicles respond differently to the
same road. The opening flat provides the simplest place to begin.

\subsection{Launch and the Shape of the Shift Curve}
\label{sec:results_launch_upshift}

Once the belt is engaged and the primary leaves the low-ratio seat, the
opening flat places relatively little disturbance on the shift. It therefore
provides a clear view of a familiar tuning observable: primary speed as a
function of secondary speed. In tuning practice, this shift curve is a familiar observable
\citep{Aaen2007,Skinner2020}, but the curve alone does not identify the
hardware balance that produced it. Here it is instead a consequence of the
actuator geometry, spring loading, belt and secondary reactions, and vehicle
acceleration solved together. This makes it possible to separate three
different things that hardware can change: the level of the working-speed
band, the way that speed changes as the shift progresses, and the part of the
shift over which the change occurs.

\Cref{fig:results_shift_curve_shape} compares six configurations while they are
freely shifting on the opening flat with both belt contacts sticking. The
reference curve is nearly level over most of the active travel. Reducing the
replaceable flyweight tip mass to \(85\%\) in W85 moves the trajectory upward:
between \(20\%\) and \(80\%\) of the available engaged shift travel, primary
speed changes from approximately \(3393\) to \(3447~\mathrm{rpm}\), compared
with approximately \(3169\) to \(3205~\mathrm{rpm}\) for R00. The shape is not
identical, but the dominant change is the higher working-speed band.

\begin{figure}[tbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.94\linewidth]{figures/results/course/opening_free_shift_characteristics.png}
  \caption{Opening-flat free-shift characteristics for selected primary tuning
  changes. Each curve is the solved primary--secondary speed trajectory while
  the CVT is engaged, freely shifting, and in stick--stick contact; no speed
  relation is prescribed. W85 primarily raises the working-speed band, P300
  progressively raises primary speed as the shift proceeds, RC10 and R26B7
  produce strongly rising and falling characteristics, and RC40L preserves the
  early response before changing the late-shift shape.}
  \label{fig:results_shift_curve_shape}
\end{figure}

The direction of the W85 change follows the primary force balance developed in
\Cref{sec:primary_flyweight_closing_force}. The leading speed-dependent
flyweight contribution in \Cref{eq:primary_flyweight_axial_force} contains
\(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_p^2\,dJ_{f,p}/dx_p\). Removing tip mass reduces the
centrifugal closing action available at a given primary speed. The coupled
balance therefore reaches comparable closing action at a higher primary speed,
while the remaining forces and inertial terms determine the exact trajectory.
The result is the familiar effect of flyweight mass, but here the speed change
is obtained from the complete transient balance rather than imposed as a
shift-speed rule.

P300 changes a different part of that balance. Its spring stiffness is three
times the reference value, while its installed compression is reduced so that
\Cref{eq:primary_spring_force} gives the same spring opening force as R00 at
the low-ratio engagement geometry. The two springs therefore begin from the
same force at that selected position, but they cannot remain equal as the
primary closes: the opening force grows with slope \(k_p\). This separation is
visible in \Cref{fig:results_shift_curve_mechanics}(a). Correspondingly, P300
is still close to R00 near the beginning of active shift, but primary speed
rises to approximately \(3336~\mathrm{rpm}\) by \(80\%\) travel. Matching one
spring force has not matched the force characteristic. Flyweight mass and
spring rate are therefore not interchangeable ways of asking the primary to
operate at a different speed.

The ramp cases change the conversion between flyweight motion and axial
closing force itself. RC10 and R26B7 preserve the first \SI{10}{mm} of the
reference ramp, whereas RC40L preserves the first \SI{18}{mm}; the modified
profiles are shown in \Cref{fig:results_shift_curve_mechanics}(b). Because the
roller initially encounters the same geometry, the early trajectories of
these cases remain close to R00. Once the roller reaches the modified region,
the geometry changes the flyweight pose and the derivative of its shaft-axis
inertia with sheave position. It also changes the motion-ratio and inertial
terms retained in the full flyweight model. Panel (c) isolates the leading
speed-dependent contribution, showing how the same
\(\omega_p^2 dJ_{f,p}/dx_p\) term acquires a different dependence on shift;
the complete trajectory still uses every term in the dynamic actuator balance.

\begin{figure}[tbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/results/course/shift_curve_mechanical_causes.png}
  \caption{Mechanical origin of the selected shift-curve changes. (a) R00 and
  P300 have the same primary spring force at the selected low-ratio engagement
  geometry, but the greater P300 stiffness causes the opening force to grow
  more rapidly with shift. (b) The ramp cases preserve a common early profile
  and alter the later physical ramp geometry. The dashed positions mark the
  \SI{10}{mm} and \SI{18}{mm} ramp coordinates at which the selected modified
  families begin to depart from the reference construction. (c) The changed
  ramp geometry produces different centrifugal closing contributions at the
  same \(3000~\mathrm{rpm}\) primary speed. The ramp changes also alter the
  retained motion-ratio and flyweight-inertia terms; panel (c) isolates the
  leading speed-dependent contribution. These maps are mechanism
  characteristics, while the speed trajectories in
  \Cref{fig:results_shift_curve_shape} are obtained only after the complete CVT
  and vehicle balances are solved.}
  \label{fig:results_shift_curve_mechanics}
\end{figure}

The resulting speed curves separate in opposite directions. In RC10, the
reduced late-shift centrifugal contribution is accompanied by a primary-speed
rise from approximately \(3169\) to
\(3655~\mathrm{rpm}\) between \(20\%\) and \(80\%\) travel. R26B7 develops a
larger speed-dependent closing contribution over the same region while its
primary speed falls to approximately \(3013~\mathrm{rpm}\). RC40L delays the geometric change
farther into the ramp. Its trajectory is essentially coincident with R00 at
\(20\%\) and \(50\%\) travel, then develops a distinct late-shift shoulder and
downturn. Thus a ramp need not simply move the complete shift curve up or down;
it can change the portion of the operating range over which the primary speed
changes.

\begin{table}[tbp]
  \centering\small
  \setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
  \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.12}
  \caption{Selected opening-flat shift-curve measures. Active shift fraction is
  the normalized engaged travel between the low-ratio seat and upper stop. The
  reported slope is the secant in primary speed with respect to secondary speed
  between \(20\%\) and \(80\%\) travel; it describes these trajectories rather
  than a prescribed or universal shift law.}
  \label{tab:results_shift_curve_metrics}
  \begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrrr@{}}
    \toprule
    Case & \shortstack{Primary rpm\\at 20\%} & \shortstack{Primary rpm\\at 50\%} &
      \shortstack{Primary rpm\\at 80\%} & \shortstack{Secant slope\\[rpm/1000 rpm]} &
      \shortstack{Opening-flat\\upper stop [m]} \\
    \midrule
    R00   & 3168.5 & 3127.4 & 3204.7 &  23.7 & 64.92 \\
    W85   & 3392.9 & 3355.5 & 3447.1 &  32.9 & 84.46 \\
    P300  & 3189.5 & 3203.5 & 3336.2 &  89.6 & 77.52 \\
    RC10  & 3168.5 & 3177.3 & 3655.4 & 250.8 & -- \\
    R26B7 & 3168.5 & 3146.6 & 3013.5 &-114.8 & 39.83 \\
    RC40L & 3168.5 & 3127.4 & 3160.0 &  -5.8 & 29.39 \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

The shape of the curve also changes which mechanical restriction is reached
later in the drive. R26B7 and RC40L arrive at the upper stop after only about
\SI{39.8}{m} and \SI{29.4}{m}, respectively, whereas R00 reaches it near
\SI{64.9}{m} and W85 near \SI{84.5}{m}. RC10 does not reach the upper stop at
all on the opening flat; its first arrival occurs much later on the descent,
near \SI{652.2}{m}.
These are not independent settings of ``shift-out distance.'' They are the
vehicle-level consequences of the different actuator characteristics in
\Cref{fig:results_shift_curve_mechanics} acting through the same coupled CVT.

A more extreme mass change provides a useful limit on that interpretation.
U55 reduces the replaceable tip mass to \(55\%\) of the reference value and
reaches only about \SI{14.08}{mm} before the severe hill interrupts the
opening acceleration. That observation alone cannot distinguish a delayed
upshift from a persistent interior operating condition. The separate
\SI{800}{m} flat run provides the needed time and distance: R00 reaches the
\SI{19.05}{mm} upper stop near \SI{64.9}{m}, while U55 remains near
\SI{14.90}{mm} through the end of the run, varying by less than
\SI{0.001}{mm} over the final \SI{10}{s}. The finite run does not establish
that U55 could never reach the stop, but it does show that incomplete upshift
on the common course is not simply an artefact of the first hill arriving a
few metres too early.

Taken together, the opening-flat cases separate three ideas that are easily
collapsed into a single notion of ``shift RPM.'' Flyweight mass can move the
working-speed band, spring rate can make the required speed diverge as the
primary closes, and ramp geometry can shape where and in which direction the
speed changes. The same hardware then remains in the transmission as the road
load increases; the following climb asks whether those different trajectories
remain merely different operating choices or become mechanical limitations.


\chapter{Conclusion and Future Work}
\label{sec:conclusion_and_future_work}

\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

TODO


\section{Future Work}
\label{sec:future_work}

The present formulation follows how the shafts, actuators, and belt respond
together as the CVT changes operating condition. Its assumptions also identify
where that description can be extended. Some omitted forces can enter the
existing free bodies; other changes would give the belt motions and
deformations that its present coordinates cannot describe. The questions
below concern what those changes would allow us to explain about the
transient response.

Much of the local physics already has a foundation in the literature reviewed
in \Cref{sec:literature-review}. Bringing it into the present problem requires
keeping the interaction between belt loading and hardware motion: a change
in contact force changes how the sheaves accelerate, and their motion changes
the contact again. The proposed extensions examine that interaction alongside
the measurements needed to distinguish its physical causes.

\subsection{Actuator Friction and Directional Shifting}
\label{sec:future_work_mechanism_friction}

The same mechanism position need not produce the same clamping force while
the CVT shifts in opposite directions. Acceleration can already contribute to
this distinction in the present model because part of the applied loading
accelerates the actuator itself. Friction in the ramps, helix, and guides
would add resistance that changes direction with the relative motion. Static
friction could also delay the start of motion or hold a sheave away from a
travel stop. These effects are excluded by
\AssRef{assump:ideal_actuator_mechanics}; their forces would enter the primary
free body and secondary helix balance in
\Cref{eq:primary_movable_sheave_balance,eq:secondary_reacted_helix_torque_structure}.

Practical tuning literature gives this question a direct motivation.
\citet{Aaen2007} describes how reversal of friction at the secondary cam and
sliding buttons delays backshift and allows engine speed to fall.
\citet{KimEtAl2002} include friction in their mechanical-actuator relations,
while \citet{Cammalleri2005} distinguishes its direction for incipient upshift
and downshift. Incorporating these forces into the dynamic balances would
allow their contribution to the separation between upshift and backshift
curves to be examined during complete maneuvers, including the recovery of
engine speed after a load increase.

That separation also depends on belt contact and inertia. Comparisons at
different shift rates, with actuator friction characterised independently,
could establish how much each contributes to delayed reversal. For the
roller mechanisms considered here, the relevant resistance may come from
roller bearings and rolling contact rather than the sliding buttons discussed
by Aaen. In either case, friction changes the contact reaction that generates
the clamping force, so it must participate in the actuator balance.

\subsection{Local Belt Motion and Contact}
\label{sec:future_work_local_contact}

The belt contact supplies both the torque carried by a pulley and the load
that opposes its actuator. The present wrap equations resolve tension and
normal loading, but one traction utilisation \(\lambda_j\) and one contact
state describe each wrap
(\Cref{eq:lambda_local_definition} and
\AssRef{assump:wrap_contact_resolution}). They cannot distinguish a region
where the belt adheres from another where it slides, or a region where the
local friction direction reverses. Resolving these motions would change the
forces returned to the sheaves as well as the description of slip.

The mechanisms identified by \citet{Gerbert1996} explain why these distinctions
matter. Longitudinal extension changes the motion of the tension-carrying
layer, and radial compression changes its running radius. Shear and warping
allow the rubber between that layer and the sheave to deform, while the
change of curvature at entry and exit changes the motion of the contacting
surface. A difference between cord speed and pulley surface speed therefore
need not imply sliding: the rubber can deform in shear while its surface
adheres. Gerbert also shows how seating and unseating can produce friction
opposing the main torque-transmitting direction at the primary. A single
wrap-level slip speed cannot recover these local motions.

Radial migration makes the direction of friction particularly important.
Traction along a sheave face can have both radial and circumferential
components. Resistance to changing running radius and resistance to
tangential slip therefore share the available contact traction.
\citet[p.~645]{KimEtAl2002} use this connection to explain the different
coefficients in their experimentally identified upshift and downshift laws.
Their proposed pseudo-self-locking mechanism occurs at the primary
belt--groove interface: outward radial friction during downshift can support
a condition resembling steady running, whereas its reversal during upshift
changes the radial and circumferential sliding. This is a belt-contact
contribution to directional shifting, separate from the actuator friction
discussed above.

The distributed groove mechanics of \citet{KongParker2006} and shifting
analysis of \citet{Sorge2008} provide foundations for determining the friction
direction from local relative motion. Such a description would replace the
circumferential-only reduction in
\AssRef{assump:gross_coulomb_traction} and determine how radial resistance,
torque transmission, and axial thrust change together during shifting. Local
motion also depends on the belt description. The inextensible continuum
kinematics of \citet{CarboneMangialardiMantriota2005} already allow local
transport to vary with changing geometry; longitudinal creep and
shear-mediated surface motion require the corresponding deformation.
A pressure- or speed-dependent friction law could be examined within the
present reduced model, but separating adhering and sliding regions would
require spatial contact resolution.

The same local mechanics would give a more detailed account of belt losses.
\citet{Bertini2014} distinguish sliding, material hysteresis, and losses at
entry to and exit from the pulley wraps. Those entry and exit processes occur
as the belt circulates, even after initial clutching is complete. Following
them during a transient would allow their effect on power loss to be examined
alongside their effect on clamp demand and shift timing.

\subsection{Face Torque and Dynamic Helix Response}
\label{sec:future_work_face_torque}

The two secondary faces provide a direct connection between local contact and
the complete transient response. The movable face rotates relative to the
shaft as it shifts, so the belt generally encounters a different surface
velocity on each face. The current formulation retains this relative rotation
in the actuator and shaft dynamics and uses the power-equivalent contact
speed in \Cref{eq:representative_contact_angular_speed}. What it does not
determine is the division of torque between the two contacts:
\AssRef{assump:symmetric_face_sharing} supplies
\(\tau_{s,M}=\tau_s/2\) in
\Cref{eq:secondary_movable_face_belt_torque}.

\citet{SorgeCammalleri2011} show why this division deserves closer attention.
Their separate face-contact calculations produce substantially unequal
torques, with circumferential tractions that can oppose one another over
parts of the wrap. In some calculated conditions, even the integrated torque
on the faster half-pulley is negative. Their analysis retains the contact
consequences of relative face motion while neglecting inertia other than
centrifugal loading. The present formulation retains the movable assembly's
inertial demand but reduces the face contacts. The two descriptions address
different consequences of the same motion.

The point at which they meet is explicit in
\Cref{eq:secondary_reacted_helix_torque_structure}. The helix reacts the belt
and spring torques remaining after the movable assembly has been accelerated.
Replacing the prescribed half-share by the actual movable-face torque changes
that reaction and hence the axial force in
\Cref{eq:secondary_helix_closing_force}. The changed acceleration alters the
relative face motion, which changes the contact conditions that supplied the
torque. The shaft balance responds at the same time. Contact power must also
follow each face torque at its own speed, replacing the equal-share reduction
in \Cref{eq:secondary_face_tangential_power}.

The transient sensitivity examined in \Cref{sec:results_secondary_helix}
makes this coupling worth investigating during smooth shifting, rapid
backshift, and torque reversal. Comparing separate face contacts with and
without the retained helix dynamics would show whether their interaction
changes the shift trajectory or the approach to a contact or support
transition. The separate torques and helix reaction would reveal changes
that total transmitted torque alone could conceal. This would establish
when the known unequal-sharing mechanics alter the dynamic response, and
when the present aggregate description remains sufficient.

\subsection{Secondary Contact and Clearance}
\label{sec:future_work_secondary_contact}

The helix reaction also raises a question about the contact that carries it.
The reference mechanism uses the zero-clearance bilateral slot described in
\Cref{app:reference_helix_topology}. A change in signed reaction is carried
immediately by the opposite flank. A one-sided helix could instead separate,
while finite clearance in a slot would allow relative motion before the
opposite flank is reached. The reaction changes examined in
\Cref{sec:results_secondary_hybrid_response} motivate studying how these
arrangements change the response to reversal.

While flank contact is absent, the movable assembly's rotation and axial
motion can no longer be tied by the same helix map. Allowing those motions
to evolve independently would determine when contact returns and how clamp
develops during the interval. This extends the mechanism's permitted motion;
an added friction force alone cannot supply it. The opening of a helix
contact remains distinct from belt lift-off, which depends on the normal
loading at the belt--sheave interface.

\subsection{Belt Deformation and Material History}
\label{sec:future_work_belt_material}

Local deformation also changes how the belt couples the two pulleys. With
the fixed length of \AssRef{assump:constant_belt_length}, the compatibility
relation in \Cref{eq:belt_length_compatibility} determines the secondary
position from the shared shift coordinate. An extensible belt could
accommodate part of a sheave displacement through elongation instead.
Tension would depend on deformation, and the position map in
\Cref{eq:secondary_coordinate_map} would no longer supply the complete
coupling. Independent sheave coordinates may then be needed, with each
movable assembly responding to its own force balance.

The single transport coordinate in \AssRef{assump:single_belt_speed} is a
related reduction. A local velocity field would allow tension changes and
deformation to propagate around the loop. This would make it possible to
examine how compliance changes the onset of shifting, transient slip, and
the transmission of a sudden load between the pulleys. It would also help
investigate the different clamp-to-shift responses identified in
\Cref{sec:results_ballew_interpretation}, without assigning that discrepancy
to one untested material mechanism.

Ballew already evolves belt-node motion and both pulley speeds together. His
running-radius search determines geometry by matching belt axial reaction
to applied clamp, rather than advancing movable-sheave axial inertia
\citep{Ballew2015}. Coupling those belt mechanics to an axial equation of
motion would allow the sheave and actuator inertia to participate in the
response. Steady descriptions require a different extension: Messick's
constant-mass-flow and steady-path assumptions would need to be reconsidered
when deformation and transport evolve through time \citep{Messick2018}.

The belt section determines which deformations can participate. Under
\AssRef{assump:fixed-belt-cross-section}, the cord location and section
geometry are prescribed. Resolving compression, bulging, and shear would
allow belt width and contact shape to change, altering the relation between
sheave position and running radius. Bending stiffness would affect entry and
exit from the wraps. A refined cord and rubber mass distribution could also
improve the inertial description without resolving every local deformation.
The material and component tests described by \citet{Ballew2015} and
\citet{Messick2018} provide foundations for characterising these responses
independently of a complete CVT test.

Retaining their influence need not require every cord or cog to be resolved
around the loop. \citet{Gerbert1996} uses sectional analysis to construct an
effective shear relation. Similar reductions could carry sectional forces,
moments, contact geometry, and material history into a belt model. A section
may deform enough to change contact even when its internal motion is fast
enough to be treated without a separate inertial state. Whether deformation,
dissipation, and sectional inertia matter are therefore related but distinct
questions.

Elasticity stores and returns energy. Viscoelasticity adds dependence on
deformation history and can dissipate energy during repeated stretching,
compression, shear, and bending. Messick measures temperature- and
frequency-dependent rubber properties but uses an elastic approximation in
the mechanical model; Ballew identifies independent damping measurements as
unfinished work \citep{Messick2018,Ballew2015}. Cyclic material or component
tests would support a transient constitutive description. Its history would
follow each belt section through the spans and wraps, while the energy
accounting in \Cref{eq:verification_energy_balance} would extend to
recoverable strain energy and material dissipation. This distinction matters
when a transient reduction in output power reflects energy being stored in
the belt.

Deformation and inertia would also permit belt vibration to be examined.
\citet{Messick2018} identifies crank-angle-dependent engine torque as an
excitation that his steady model cannot predict. A transient belt model
could determine whether torque ripple materially changes contact loading
and traction. Physical vibration would need to
remain distinguishable from numerical oscillation through spatial and
temporal refinement, particularly given the stiffness difficulties reported
by \citet{Ballew2015}.

\subsection{Belt Path, Support, and Re-engagement}
\label{sec:future_work_belt_path}

A deformable belt also raises a more basic geometric question: which path
the belt follows through the machine. The circular wraps and straight spans
of \AssRef{assump:prescribed_belt_path} remove possible motions before the
force balances are solved. The checks in
\Cref{eq:wrap_contact_admissibility} can identify a negative tension or local
normal load. They cannot determine the slack or partially detached
configuration that would replace the assumed path. Determining belt position
and contact extent from the mechanics would allow the model to continue
through such conditions.

This would be especially useful while the primary is disengaged. Under
\AssRef{assump:deadzone_belt_closure}, the belt is carried without slip by the
fully closed secondary through \Cref{eq:deadzone_secondary_lock}. A belt with
its own deformation and contact motion could instead determine installed
pretension, slack take-up, support at the primary hub, intermittent sheave
contact, and possible secondary slip. Initial engagement would then depend
on the belt configuration and motion actually reached. These are changes to
the physical description of engagement, beyond refining its event time.

There is substantial precedent for solving belt paths and contact boundaries
\citep{KongParker2005,KongParker2006,JulioPlante2011}. The question here is how
that freedom interacts with mechanically determined actuation through
disengagement and re-engagement. The difficulty reported by
\citet{JulioPlante2011} in obtaining stabilised predictions near the
clutching-to-shifting transition makes low-load engagement a particularly
useful test of the resulting description.

Planar motion may be sufficient for some studies of tension redistribution
and slackness. Axial migration, skew, and section twist require out-of-plane
motion, and an axially floating secondary introduces translation distinct
from opening its groove. Pulley compliance and shaft misalignment are
separate extensions of \AssRef{assump:rigid_hardware} and
\AssRef{assump:axis_alignment}. The metal-belt and chain-CVT studies of
\citet{CarboneMangialardiMantriota2005} and \citet{DuanEtAl2016} show that
hardware deformation can alter contact; its importance relative to rubber
deformation would need to be established for the loads considered here.

\subsection{Friction and Compliance During Finite-Speed Events}
\label{sec:future_work_events}

When engagement or a travel stop imposes a new constraint at finite relative
speed, the impulse--momentum balance in
\Cref{eq:finite_event_generalized_impulse_momentum} determines the resulting
velocity change. This compact treatment allows the simulation to continue
through the event. Two extensions would examine which contact details can
change its outcome.

The first concerns friction during the instantaneous event. Under
\AssRef{assump:impact_tangential_contact}, an existing sticking belt contact
transmits the impulse required to remain stuck without a separate friction
capacity check. An impulse-level traction law coupled to the normal impulse
could determine whether sticking can be maintained or the event produces
tangential slip. The outgoing constraints in
\Cref{eq:finite_event_outgoing_constraints} would then depend on the
admissible contact impulses, following the frictional-impact framework
discussed by \citet{Stewart2000RigidImpact}.

The second concerns contact duration. The instantaneous, perfectly inelastic
treatment in \AssRef{assump:inelastic_constraint_activation} determines an
outgoing motion and energy change, but cannot resolve peak force, rebound,
or vibration during capture. Advancing selected compliant contacts through
time would make those quantities accessible. Friction-limited impulses and
finite-duration contact therefore answer different questions: whether the
assumed outgoing stick is supportable, and whether the contact's deformation
and duration materially affect the response. The instantaneous description
may remain sufficient for the overall shift trajectory even where resolving
peak loads requires compliance.

\subsection{Temperature During Transient Operation}
\label{sec:future_work_temperature}

The losses in the belt and its contacts can also change the properties that
determine the subsequent motion. Under
\AssRef{assump:material_surface_state}, those properties remain fixed
throughout a run. The measurements of \citet{Messick2018} show that rubber
stiffness and dissipation depend on temperature and loading frequency. The
same maneuver may therefore produce a different response from a cold or
warm belt. The question is whether fixed properties at the measured initial
temperature are sufficient, or whether heating during the maneuver changes
the mechanics enough to require an evolving temperature.

The temperature of the belt as a whole is only part of the question. Local
contact can heat over much shorter distances and times than the bulk
material. \citet{Persson2006FlashTemperature} describes how flash heating
feeds back into rubber friction through the temperature-dependent material
response. For a CVT, clutching, brief slip, and traction recovery give this
mechanism a specific transient setting: recent sliding may change the force
available as contact recovers. Its importance at the belt--sheave interface
remains to be established. A nearly unchanged bulk temperature during a
short maneuver would not, by itself, show that flash heating is negligible.

\subsection{Parasitic Component Losses}
\label{sec:future_work_component_losses}

Bearing, seal, and windage resistance provide a smaller extension of
\AssRef{assump:no_parasitic_losses}. Attaching independently characterised
losses to the bodies and relative motions that produce them would allow
their effect on acceleration and efficiency to be examined alongside the
belt losses. The shaft interface in \Cref{eq:shaft_boundary_interface}
identifies which belong inside the CVT and which are supplied by the
attached machine. This gives a physical allocation of the losses without
introducing a single efficiency correction for the complete transmission.

\subsection{Experimental Validation and Identification of Physical Causes}
\label{sec:future_work_validation}

The operating regions represented by the model offer different opportunities
to examine its mechanics. At a travel stop, shift motion is suppressed, so
loading can be varied while the pulley geometry remains fixed. Free shifting
then brings the axial dynamics into the comparison. Supported operation also
introduces a reaction: the belt load need not equal the actuator force because
the stop carries the remainder of the axial balance.
\citet{GrzegozekDobajKot2016} identify this limitation when inferring belt
loads from regulator characteristics. The reactions in
\Cref{tab:structural_stop_reactions} therefore require measurements beyond
the actuator law if supported operation is to test the predicted belt loading.

Measurements during shifting should also include more than shaft speeds.
In the model comparison of \Cref{sec:results_ballew_closed_loop}, feedback
approaches the reference shaft histories while producing a substantially
different primary clamp. Replaying the reference force in
\Cref{sec:results_ballew_force_replay} removes that adjustment and exposes a
different response. Sheave displacement distinguishes geometric shifting
from the speed changes produced by slip; belt speed, torque, and selected
actuator loads can further distinguish the causes. The thrust and torque
measurements of \citet{Bertini2014} provide a useful precedent. For the
secondary coupling discussed above, movable-face torque or helix reaction
would be more informative than total shaft torque alone. A force supplied
as an input must remain distinct from one predicted and measured as an output.

An inertia dynamometer offers a practical first transient test. A
characterised inertia connected to the secondary constrains the torque
received through its acceleration once rig losses are accounted for. With
a measured or independently characterised primary torque boundary, the
model could predict shaft and sheave histories through engagement,
low-ratio acceleration, and upshift. Controlled load changes would extend
the comparison to backshift and traction recovery through the same shaft
interfaces. Together, these cases would test the progression between
operating regions as well as the response within each one.

The independent material and component characterisation described above
would let these experiments test the coupled response without reselecting
parameters for each maneuver. Comparisons of belt descriptions under common
shaft loading and matched initial conditions, as far as their states permit,
could then distinguish the effects of deformation, dissipation, contact, and
path freedom. This would turn differences such as the clamp-to-shift
discrepancy in \Cref{sec:results_ballew_interpretation} into questions that
measurements can help resolve.

\subsection{Reduced Models and Design Studies}
\label{sec:future_work_reductions_design}

The same investigations could establish where a simpler description is
sufficient. The comparisons in \Cref{sec:results_reduced_belt_mechanics}
distinguish the size of a term on the full trajectory from the change caused
by removing it and integrating again. Extending that approach to contact
and deformation would identify which motions can be reduced while preserving
the shift response and contact transitions of interest. A reduction must
remain consistent across the balances in which the same mass or force
participates. Its usefulness can then be assessed under common loading,
with numerical refinement sufficient to resolve the difference between
the models.

Once the relevant predictions are tested, design studies can follow changes
to a ramp, flyweight, spring, or helix through the forces and inertias they
alter, building on the component-based approaches of
\citet{Cammalleri2005} and \citet{Skinner2020}. Examining engagement,
acceleration, and recovery after a load change would reveal tradeoffs that
a single target engine speed cannot describe. The question becomes whether
a component change improves the intended operating response, and whether
that improvement persists across the measured variation in the hardware.
